    var allParameters = from element in exp.Arguments
                        select processArgument(element);
    Console.WriteLine($"Calling {methodName}");

    foreach (var parm in allParameters)
        Console.WriteLine(@$"\tParameter type =
            {parm.ParmType},
            Value = {parm.ParmValue}");
    return default(TResult);
}

private (Type ParmType, object ParmValue) processArgument(
    Expression element)
{
    object argument = default(object);
    LambdaExpression expression = Expression.Lambda(
        Expression.Convert(element, element.Type));
    Type parmType = expression.ReturnType;
    argument = expression.Compile().DynamicInvoke();
    return (parmType, argument);
}

Starting from the beginning of `CallInterface`, the first thing this code does is look at the body of the expression tree—that is, the part on the right side of the lambda operator. Consider once again the example that used `CallInterface()`; it called the method with `srver.DoWork(172)`. This `MethodCallExpression` contains all the information you need to understand all the parameters and the method name invoked. The method name is really simple: It’s stored in the `Name` property of the `Method` property. In this example, the name is “DoWork.” The LINQ query processes any and all parameters to this method.

The more interesting work occurs in `processArgument`, which evaluates each parameter expression. In the earlier example, there was only one argument—a constant, the value 172\. That approach is not very robust, so the new code takes a different strategy. Any of the parameters in this example could be method calls, property or indexer accessors, or even field accessors. Any of the method calls could also contain parameters of any of those types. Instead of trying to parse everything, this method leverages the `LambdaExpression` type and evaluates each parameter expression. Every parameter expression—even the `ConstantExpression`—can be expressed as the return value from a lambda expression. `ProcessArgument()` converts the parameter to a `LambdaExpression`. In the case of the constant expression, it would convert it to a lambda expression equivalent to `() => 172`. As noted earlier, a lambda expression can be compiled into a delegate, and the delegate can then be invoked. In the case of the parameter expression, the example code creates a delegate that returns the constant value 172\. More complicated expressions could be developed to create more complicated lambda expressions.

Once the lambda expression has been created, you can retrieve the type of the parameter from the lambda. Notice that the example method does not perform any processing on the parameters. The code to evaluate the parameters in the lambda expression executes only when the lambda expression is invoked. The beauty of this approach is that the method could even contain other calls to `CallInterface()`. These kinds of constructs work like this:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p255pro01)

client.CallInterface(srver => srver.DoWork(
    client.CallInterface(srv => srv.GetANumber())));

By applying this technique, you can use expression trees to determine at runtime which code the user wishes to execute. It’s difficult to show this aspect in a book, but because `ClientProxy<T>` is a generic class that uses the service interface as a type parameter, the `CallInterface` method is strongly typed. The method call in the lambda expression must be a member method defined on the server.

The first example illustrated how you can parse expressions to convert code (or at least expressions that define code) into data elements that you then use to implement runtime algorithms. The second example runs in the opposite direction, covering the scenario in which you want to generate code at runtime.

One problem that must often be solved in large systems relates to the creation of an object of some destination type from some related source type. For example, your large enterprise may contain systems from different vendors, each of which has a different type defined for a contact (among other types). Sure, you could type methods by hand, but that’s tedious. It would be much better to create some kind of type that “figures out” the obvious implementation. You’d like to just write this code:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p256pro01)

var converter = new Converter<SourceContact,
    DestinationContact>();
DestinationContact dest2 = converter.ConvertFrom(source);

As part of this solution, you would expect the converter to copy every property from the source to the destination, so that the properties have the same name, and so that the source object has a public `get` accessor and the destination type has a public `set` accessor. This kind of runtime code generation can be best handled by creating an expression, and then compiling and executing it. You want to generate code that does something like this:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p256pro02)

// Not legal C#; for explanation only
TDest ConvertFromImaginary(TSource source)
{
    TDest destination = new TDest();
    foreach (var prop in sharedProperties)
        destination.prop = source.prop;
    return destination;
}

You need to create an expression that creates code that, in turn, executes the pseudocode given earlier. Here’s the full method to create that expression and compile it to a function. Immediately following the listing, we’ll examine all the parts of this method in detail. It’s a bit thorny at first, but nothing you can’t handle.

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p256pro03)

private void createConverterIfNeeded()
{
    if (converter == null)
    {
        var source = Expression.Parameter(typeof(TSource),
            "source");
        var dest = Expression.Variable(typeof(TDest), "dest");

        var assignments = from srcProp in
            typeof(TSource).GetProperties(
            BindingFlags.Public | BindingFlags.Instance)
               where srcProp.CanRead
               let destProp = typeof(TDest).GetProperty(
                   srcProp.Name,
                   BindingFlags.Public |
                   BindingFlags.Instance)
               where (destProp != null) && (destProp.CanWrite)
               select Expression.Assign(
                   Expression.Property(dest, destProp),
                   Expression.Property(source, srcProp));

        // Put together the body:
        var body = new List<Expression>();
        body.Add(Expression.Assign(dest,
            Expression.New(typeof(TDest))));
        body.AddRange(assignments);
        body.Add(dest);

        var expr =
            Expression.Lambda<Func<TSource, TDest>>(
                Expression.Block(
                new[] { dest }, // Expression parameters
                body.ToArray() // Body
                ),
                source  // Lambda expression
            );

        var func = expr.Compile();
        converter = func;
    }
}

This method creates code that mimics the pseudocode shown earlier. First, you declare the parameter:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p257pro01)

var source = Expression.Parameter(typeof(TSource), "source");

Then, you declare a local variable to hold the destination:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p257pro02)

var dest = Expression.Variable(typeof(TDest), "dest");

The bulk of the method is the code that assigns properties from the source object to the destination object—here written as a LINQ query. The source sequence of the LINQ query is the set of all public instance properties in the source object for which there is a `get` accessor:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p258pro01)

from srcProp in typeof(TSource).GetProperties(
                BindingFlags.Public | BindingFlags.Instance)
                where srcProp.CanRead

The `let` statement declares a local variable that holds the property of the same name in the destination type. It may be null, if the destination type does not have a property of the correct type:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p258pro02)

let destProp = typeof(TDest).GetProperty(
               srcProp.Name,
               BindingFlags.Public | BindingFlags.Instance)
        where (destProp != null) && (destProp.CanWrite)

The projection of the query is a sequence of assignment statements that assigns the property of the destination object to the value of the same property name in the source object:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p258pro03)

select Expression.Assign(
       Expression.Property(dest, destProp),
       Expression.Property(source, srcProp));

The rest of the method builds the body of the lambda expression. The `Block()` method of the `Expression` class needs all the statements in an array of expressions. The next step is to create a `List<Expression>` where you can add all the statements. The list can be easily converted to an array.

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p258pro04)

var body = new List<Expression>();
body.Add(Expression.Assign(dest,
    Expression.New(typeof(TDest))));
body.AddRange(assignments);
body.Add(dest);

Finally, it’s time to build a lambda expression that returns the destination object and contains all the statements built so far:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p258pro05)

var expr =
    Expression.Lambda<Func<TSource, TDest>>(
        Expression.Block(
        new[] { dest }, // Expression parameters
        body.ToArray() // Body
        ),
        source  // Lambda expression
    );

That’s all the code you need. Now it’s time to compile it and turn it into a delegate that you can call:

var func = expr.Compile();
converter = func;

This example is complicated, and the code is not especially easy to write. You’ll often find compiler-like errors continue to be generated at runtime until the expressions are built correctly. This solution is also clearly not the best way to approach simple problems. Even so, the expression APIs are much simpler than their predecessors in the reflection APIs that emit IL. That fact prompts this guideline for when you should use the expression APIs: When you think you want to use reflection, try to solve the problem using the expression APIs instead.

The expression APIs can be used in two very different ways. First, you can create methods that take expressions as parameters, which enables you to parse those expressions and create code based on the concepts behind the expressions that were called. Second, you can create code at runtime; that is, you can create classes that write code, and then execute the code they’ve written. This strategy is a very powerful way to solve some of the more difficult general-purpose problems you’ll encounter.

### Item 47: Minimize Dynamic Objects in Public APIs

Dynamic objects just don’t behave well in a statically typed system. The type system perceives them to be instances of `System.Object`, but they are actually special instances. You can ask dynamic objects to do work above and beyond what’s defined in `System.Object`. The compiler generates code that tries to find and execute whatever members you try to access.

But dynamic objects are also pushy—everything they touch becomes dynamic. Perform an operation where any one of the parameters is dynamic, and the result is dynamic. Return a dynamic object from a method, and everywhere that object is used becomes a dynamic object. It’s like watching bread mold grow in a Petri dish. Pretty soon, everything is dynamic, and there’s no type safety left anywhere.

Biologists grow cultures in Petri dishes, thereby restricting the cultured organisms’ spread. You need to do the same with dynamic typing: Do the work with dynamic objects in an isolated environment and return objects that are statically typed as something other than dynamic. Otherwise, dynamic typing exerts a bad influence, causing everything involved in your application to slowly become dynamic.

This is not to imply that dynamic programming is universally bad. Other items in this chapter cover some of the techniques where dynamic programming is an excellent solution. However, dynamic typing and static typing are very different, with different practices, different idioms, and different strategies. Mixing the two without taking due precautions will lead to numerous errors and inefficiencies. C# is a statically typed language, so it supports dynamic typing in some areas. Therefore, if you’re using C#, you should spend most of your time using static typing and minimize the scope of the dynamic features. If you want to write programs that are dynamic through and through, you should consider a language that is dynamic rather than statically typed.

If you plan to use dynamic features in your program, try to keep them out of the public interface to your types. That way, you can use dynamic typing in a single object (or type) Petri dish, yet prevent the dynamic typing from infecting the rest of your program, or all the code developed by developers who use your objects.

One scenario in which you will use dynamic typing is to interact with objects created in dynamic environments, such as IronPython. When your design makes use of dynamic objects created using dynamic languages, you should wrap them in C# objects; such objects enable the rest of the C# world to blissfully ignore the fact that dynamic typing is even happening.

You may want to pick a different solution for those situations in which you use dynamic typing to produce duck typing. Look at the uses of duck typing in [Item 43](#ch05.xhtml_ch05lev1sec1). In every case, the result of the calculation was dynamic. That might not look too bad, but you should realize that the compiler is doing quite a bit of work in such cases. These two lines of code (see [Item 43](#ch05.xhtml_ch05lev1sec1))

dynamic answer = Add(5, 5);
Console.WriteLine(answer);

turn into this to handle dynamic objects:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p260pro01)

// Compiler generated; not legal user C# code
object answer = Add(5, 5);
if (<Main>o__SiteContainer0.<>p__Site1 == null)
{
    <Main>o__SiteContainer0.<>p__Site1 =
        CallSite<Action<CallSite, Type, object>>.Create(
        new CSharpInvokeMemberBinder(
        CSharpCallFlags.None, "WriteLine",
        typeof(Program), null, new CSharpArgumentInfo[]
        {
            new CSharpArgumentInfo(
            CSharpArgumentInfoFlags.IsStaticType |
            CSharpArgumentInfoFlags.UseCompileTimeType,
            null),
            new CSharpArgumentInfo(
                CSharpArgumentInfoFlags.None,
            null)
        }));
}
<Main>o__SiteContainer0.<>p__Site1.Target.Invoke(
   <Main>o__SiteContainer0.<>p__Site1,
   typeof(Console), answer);

Dynamic typing is not free. The compiler must generate quite a bit of code to make dynamic invocation work in C#. Even worse, this code will be repeated everywhere that you invoke the dynamic `Add()` method—and that repetition will have size and performance implications for your application.

You can wrap the `Add()` method shown in [Item 43](#ch05.xhtml_ch05lev1sec1) in a bit of generic syntax to create a version that keeps the dynamic types in a constrained location. With this approach, the same code will be generated but in fewer places:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p261pro01)

    private static dynamic DynamicAdd(dynamic left,
        dynamic right) =>
        left + right;

// Wrap it:
public static T1 Add<T1, T2>(T1 left, T2 right)
{
    dynamic result = DynamicAdd(left, right);
    return (T1)result;
}

The compiler generates all the dynamic callsite code in the generic `Add()` method, which isolates it in one location. Furthermore, the callsites become quite a bit simpler. Where previously every result was dynamic, now the result is statically typed to match the type of the first argument. Of course, you can create an overload to control the result type:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p262pro01)

public static TResult Add<T1, T2, TResult>
    (T1 left, T2 right)
{
    dynamic result = DynamicAdd(left, right);
    return (TResult)result;
}

In either case, the callsites live completely in the strongly typed world:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p262pro02)

int answer = Add(5, 5);
Console.WriteLine(answer);

double answer2 = Add(5.5, 7.3);
Console.WriteLine(answer2);

// Type arguments needed because
// args are not the same type
answer2 = Add<int, double, double>(5, 12.3);
Console.WriteLine(answer);

string stringLabel = System.Convert.ToString(answer);

string label = Add("Here is ", "a label");
Console.WriteLine(label);

DateTime tomorrow = Add(DateTime.Now, TimeSpan.FromDays(1));
Console.WriteLine(tomorrow);

label = "something" + 3;
Console.WriteLine(label);
label = Add("something", 3);
Console.WriteLine(label);

The preceding code is the same example from [Item 43](#ch05.xhtml_ch05lev1sec1), but it includes static types that are not dynamic as the return values. As a consequence, the caller does not need to work with dynamically typed objects. That is, the caller works with static types, safely ignoring the machinations you needed to perform to make the dynamic operation work. In fact, they don’t need to know that your algorithm ever left the safety of the type system.

Throughout this chapter, you have seen that dynamic types should be kept isolated to the smallest scope possible. When the code needs to use dynamic features, the examples have relied on a local variable that is dynamic. The methods then convert that dynamic object into a strongly typed object, so that the dynamic object never leaves the scope of the method. When you use a dynamic object to implement an algorithm, you can avoid having that dynamic object be part of your interface.

Sometimes, however, the very nature of the problem requires that a dynamic object be part of the interface. That is still not an excuse to make everything dynamic: Only the members that rely on dynamic behavior should use dynamic objects. You can mix dynamic and static typing in the same API, but ideally you should create code that is statically typed when possible. Use dynamic typing only when you must.

All programmers have to work with CSV data in different forms at some point. A production-hardened library is available at [https://github.com/JoshClose/CsvHelper](https://github.com/JoshClose/CsvHelper). Let’s look at a simplified implementation based on this kind of data. The following snippet of code reads two different CSV files with different headers and displays the items in each row:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p263pro01)

var data = new CSVDataContainer(
    new System.IO.StringReader(myCSV));
    foreach (var item in data.Rows)
        Console.WriteLine(@$"{item.Name}, {item.PhoneNumber},
{item.Label}");

data = new CSVDataContainer(
    new System.IO.StringReader(myCSV2));
foreach (var item in data.Rows)
    Console.WriteLine(@$"{item.Date}, {item.high},
{item.low}");

This API style is preferred for a general CSV reader class. The rows returned after enumerating the data contain properties for every row header name. Obviously, the row header names are not known at compile time, so those properties must be dynamic. But nothing else in the `CSVDataContainer` needs to be dynamic: The `CSVDataContainer` does not support dynamic typing. However, the `CSVDataContainer` does contain APIs that return a dynamic object that represents a row:

[Click here to view code image](#ch05_ima.xhtml_p264pro01)

public class CSVDataContainer
{
    private class CSVRow : DynamicObject
    {
        private List<(string, string)> values =
            new List<(string, string)>();
        public CSVRow(IEnumerable<string> headers,
            IEnumerable<string> items)
        {
            values.AddRange(headers.Zip(items,
                (header, value) => (header,
                        value)));
        }

        public override bool TryGetMember(
            GetMemberBinder binder,
            out object result)
        {
            var answer = values.FirstOrDefault(n =>
                n.Item1 == binder.Name);
            result = answer.Item2;
            return result != null;
        }
    }
    private List<string> columnNames = new List<string>();
    private List<CSVRow> data = new List<CSVRow>();

    public CSVDataContainer(System.IO.TextReader stream)
    {
        // Read headers:
        var headers = stream.ReadLine();
        columnNames =
            (from header in headers.Split(',')
                select header.Trim()).ToList();

        var line = stream.ReadLine();
        while (line != null)
        {
            var items = line.Split(',');
            data.Add(new CSVRow(columnNames, items));
            line = stream.ReadLine();
        }
    }
    public dynamic this[int index] => data[index];

    public IEnumerable<dynamic> Rows => data;
}

Even though you need to expose a dynamic type as part of your interface, that exposure occurs only where absolutely necessary. The APIs are dynamic because they must be—you can’t support any possible CSV format without having dynamic support for column names. Although you could have chosen to expose everything using dynamic typing, it appears in the interface only where the functionality demands dynamic typing.

Because of space constraints, the preceding example omitted other features in the `CSVDataContainer`. Think about how you would implement `RowCount`, `ColumnCount`, `GetAt(row, column)`, and other APIs. The implementation you have in your head would not use dynamic objects in the API, or even in the implementation. You can meet those requirements with static typing—and you should. You should use dynamic typing in the public interface only when it is truly needed.

Dynamic types are a useful feature, even in a statically typed language like C#. But recognize that C# is still a statically typed language—and realize that the majority of C# programs should make the most out of the type system provided by the language. Dynamic programming is still useful, but it’s most useful in C# when you keep it confined to those locations where it’s genuinely needed and where you convert dynamic objects into a different static type immediately. When your code relies on a dynamic type created in another environment, wrap those dynamic objects and provide a public interface using different static types.

## 6. Participate in the Global C# Community

The C# language is used by millions of developers worldwide. That community has created a body of knowledge and conventional wisdom about the language. C# questions and answers are consistently in the top ten categories on Stack Overflow. The language team contributes to this community as well, by discussing language design on GitHub. In addition, the compiler is available on an open source basis on GitHub. You need to get involved, too. Be part of the community.

### Item 48: Seek the Best Answer, Not the Most Popular Answer

One challenge for a popular programming language community is to evolve practices as newer features are added to the language. The C# team has continued to add features to the language that address idioms that were difficult to write correctly. The C# community would like to see more people adopt these newer practices. Nevertheless, a significant body of work exists that recommends the previous patterns. A large amount of code in production was written with earlier versions of the language. The examples in those products represent the best practices at the time when they were written. It also takes time for new and better techniques to become the most popular options in search engines and other sites. For that reason, you’ll often find that the most popular recommendations rely on older techniques instead of more modern and better choices.

The C# community encompasses a large and diverse set of developers. The good news is that this expansive community has made a wealth of information available to help you learn C# and improve your programming technique. Plug your question into any search engine and you’ll instantly get hundreds or thousands of answers. It takes time for great answers to generate enough popularity so that they rise to the top of the rankings. Once they have arrived on the first page of search results, the most popular answers will often crowd out newer and better answers.

The large size of the C# community means that changes in online results happen at a glacial pace. Newer developers who search for solutions are likely to find good answers that show the best ideas from two or three versions back of the language. But, in fact, the popularity of these outdated answers was often the motivation for adding new language features. The language design team evaluates new features and adds those that can have the best impact on developers’ daily work. The workarounds and extra code previously needed are often precisely why these new features have been added. The popularity of the workarounds indicates the importance of the new features; now, it’s up to us as professional developers to help popularize the best features.

One relevant example is covered in [Item 8](#ch01.xhtml_ch01lev1sec8) in *Effective C#, Third Edition*. It points out that using the `?.` operator to invoke a delegate or raise an event provides a thread-safe way to check for `null` and invoke those methods when the result is not `null`. The practice of initializing a local variable and then checking it before raising events has a long history, and this outdated practice is still the most popular approach shown on many sites.

The vast majority of the sample code and production code that works with text output uses the archaic positional syntax to perform string formatting and substitution. [Items 4](#ch01.xhtml_ch01lev1sec4) and [5](#ch01.xhtml_ch01lev1sec5) in *Effective C#, Third Edition*, cover the new interpolated string syntax.

Professional developers should do what they can to promote the best modern techniques. First and foremost, when you search for answers, look beyond the most popular answer to find the *best* answer for modern C# development. Do enough research to determine which answer is best for your environment and the platforms and versions you must support.

Second, once you find that best answer, support it. That’s how the newer answers will slowly rise to the top of the results page and become the most popular.

Third, where possible, update the pages with the more popular answers so that they reference the newer, better answer.

Finally, when you work on your own codebases, with each update, make that code better. Look at the code you’re updating and determine whether there are opportunities to refactor it to use better techniques—and then make some of those fixes. You won’t update an entire codebase in a single sprint, but small amounts of additional care will add up.

Follow those recommendations to be a good citizen, and you’ll help others in the C# community find the best, most relevant answers to modern questions.

It takes time for large communities to adopt newer and better practices. Some may move into prominence immediately, others many years later. Wherever you and your team are on the adoption curve, you can help by promoting the best answers for modern development.

### Item 49: Participate in Specs and Code

The open source nature of C# extends beyond the compiler code. The language design process is an open process as well. A wealth of resources are available to help you continue learning and growing your C# knowledge. Accessing these resources is one of the best ways for you to learn, keep up to date, and participate in the ongoing evolution of C#. Specifically, you should visit [https://github.com/dotnet/roslyn](https://github.com/dotnet/roslyn) and [https://github.com/dotnet/csharplang](https://github.com/dotnet/csharplang) on a regular basis.

This type of collaboration represents a major change in the C# community, but offers a new way for you to learn and interact with your fellow developers. There are many ways for you to get involved and grow.

C# is an open source language, so you can build the compiler yourself, and run your own C# compiler. You can submit changes to the language developers, and even suggest your own features and enhancements. If you have great ideas, you can prototype them in your fork and submit the pull request for your new feature.

That type of participation in the community may require more effort than you want to exert, but there are still plenty of other ways for you to become involved. If you find any issues, report them on the Roslyn repository at GitHub. The open issues identified there indicate the work that the C# team is doing or has planned. The issues encompass suspected bugs, spec issues, new feature requests, and ongoing updates to the language.

You can also find specifications for any new features on the CSharpLang repository. Those specs are your way to learn about upcoming features, and to participate in the design and evolution of the language. All of the specs are open for comment and discussion. Read the thoughts of other community members, participate in the discussions, and take an interest in the evolution of your favorite language. New specifications are posted as they are ready for review, and the cadence varies along the release cycle. More specs are published early in the planning phases for a new release, and fewer as a release nears its final stages. Even during the early phases, you can keep up by reading one or two feature specs each week. References to the specifications are found in the “Proposals” folder on the CSharpLang repository. Each proposal has a “champion” issue that tracks its status. These issues are also where you can comment on active proposals.

In addition to the specifications, the minutes from the language design meetings are posted to the CSharpLang repository. Reading those documents will give you deeper insight into the considerations behind any new features in the language. You’ll learn the constraints involved in adding any new feature to a mature language. The language design team discusses the positive and negative impacts of any new feature, weighs the benefits, outlines the schedule, and indicates the impact. You’ll learn about the scenarios that are anticipated for each new feature. These notes are also open for comment and discussion. Add your voice, and take an interest in the evolution of the language. The language design team meets roughly once a month, and the notes are often published shortly after each meeting. Reading the notes won’t take much of your time, and it’s well worth the investment. The meeting minutes are published in the “Meetings” folder on the CSharpLang repository.

The C# language specification has been converted to markdown, and is also stored on the CSharpLang repository. Comment, write issues, or even issue a pull request (PR) if you find mistakes.

If you feel more adventurous, clone the Roslyn repository and look at the unit tests for the compiler. You can explore in more depth the rules that govern any of the features in the language.

It’s a big change for the C# community to move from a closed source implementation, in which only a small percentage of the community had early access and could participate in the discussions, to an open source implementation that is available to the entire C# community. You should participate. Learn more about the features coming in the next releases. Get actively involved where you have greatest interest.

### Item 50: Consider Automating Practices with Analyzers

Both *Effective C#, Third Edition*, and this book contain recommendations for writing better code. Implementing many of these recommendations can be supported with the application of analyzers and code fixes built using the Roslyn APIs. These APIs enable add-ins to analyze code at a semantic level and modify that code to introduce better practices.

The good news is that you may not need to write those analyzers yourself. Several open source projects provide analyzers and code fixes for a variety of practices.

One very popular project is built by the Roslyn compiler team. The Roslyn Analyzers project ([https://github.com/dotnet/roslyn-analyzers](https://github.com/dotnet/roslyn-analyzers)) started as a way for the team to validate the static analysis APIs. It has since grown to include automatic validation for many common guidelines used by the Roslyn teams.

Another popular library is the Code Cracker project ([https://github.com/code-cracker/code-cracker](https://github.com/code-cracker/code-cracker)). This project is created by members of the .NET community, and reflects their thoughts on best practices for writing code. Both C# and VB.NET versions of the analyzers are available.

Another group of community members created a GitHub organization and wrote a set of analyzers to implement different recommendations. Visit the .NET Analyzers organization page ([https://github.com/DotNetAnalyzers](https://github.com/DotNetAnalyzers)) to learn more. These developers have created analyzers for different application types and libraries.

Before you install any of these analyzers, research which rules they enforce and how strident they are about enforcing those rules. Analyzers can report rule violations as information, warnings, or even errors. In addition, different analyzers may have different opinions about different rules. Rules may conflict with each other. You may find that fixing one violation triggers another analyzer to identify a violation. (For example, some analyzers prefer using implicitly typed variables, declared with `var`, whereas other analyzers prefer explicitly naming each variable’s type.) Many of the analyzers offer configuration options that enable you to decide which rules will be enforced and which will be ignored. Use those options to create the configuration that works for you. Along the way, you’ll also learn more about the rules and practices of each of the analyzers.

If you have practices that you want to follow but that aren’t represented by analyzers you can find, consider building your own. The analyzers in the Roslyn Analyzers repository are open source, and provide a great template for building analyzers. Building an analyzer is an advanced topic that requires a deep understanding of C# syntax and semantic analysis. Nevertheless, working on a simple analyzer can give you much deeper insight into the C# language. To get started, you can explore a repository I’ve used to explain the techniques. This repository, which is found on GitHub ([https://github.com/BillWagner/NonVirtualEventAnalyzer](https://github.com/BillWagner/NonVirtualEventAnalyzer)), shows you how to build an analyzer that finds and replaces virtual events (see [Item 21](#ch02.xhtml_ch02lev1sec11)). Each number branch shows one step in analyzing and fixing the code.

The Roslyn APIs for analyzers and code fixes support automated validation of any code practice you want to enforce. A rich set of analyzers has been built by the team and the community and is available for your use. If none of those analyzers meets your needs, you can work to build your own. That is an advanced technique, but it will help you gain a much deeper knowledge of the C# language rules.
